As temperatures dip and the nights draw in, why not look to your birth month winter bird to learn more about your personality?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The seasons are shifting, which means our feathered friends are about to embark upon a changing of the guard... so which is your birth month winter bird, you ask?

It's not a silly question, by any means. After all, some birds migrate south for winter, some arrive from further north, and year-round residents shift their habits. And, from the brilliant red cardinal perched against a snowy backdrop to the tiny dark-eyed junco hopping beneath a bird feeder, these seasonal visitors have plenty of personality.

With that in mind, then, what if every month had its own winter bird to call its own? What might it reveal about the people born under its wings?

Now, we’re already familiar with the idea of a classic birth month bird, not to mention the birth month owl and birth month bird of prey, but what might the unique traits that make each winter bird so wonderful say about our own personalities?

Some are bold and charismatic, others are quiet and contemplative, and a few are wonderfully independent. All, though, are beautiful in their own special way, and so much fun to watch as they go about their business over the colder months.

With that in mind, then, I've paired 12 fascinating winter birds with the personality traits commonly associated with each birth month, along with a few easy ways you can make your garden a more welcoming place for them. Time to grab yourself a motion-activated wildlife camera with night vision, like the GardePro on Amazon, then, and start reading...

If you're born in January, you're likely to be headstrong, disciplined and hardworking (all of which are, natural leadership qualities, just in case you fancied informing your boss). Naturally, then, your birth month winter bird is a wonderful reflection of that determination: the snow bunting.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

These beautiful little birds are highly social during winter, often gathering in flocks to forage for seeds in open fields and along shorelines, while their ability to thrive in bitterly cold conditions is rather impressive for such a small bird.

Like January babies, basically, they don't shy away from a challenge; they meet it head-on! To help them do this, be sure to leave native grasses and seed-producing plants standing where possible, rather than tidying every corner of your garden.

One of the most iconic birds in the world, the striking snowy owl has brilliant white plumage, piercing yellow eyes and an unmistakable presence. They spend much of the year in the Arctic, but some travel south during winter, occasionally appearing in northern parts of the United States (although they tend to favour wide-open landscapes, including coastal dunes, fields and other exposed areas where they can hunt).

Often solitary hunters, February's birth month winter bird gets by on its own instincts and remarkable ability to spot prey from a distance. It's also wonderfully adapted to its harsh environment, making it a fitting symbol of independence, deep-thinking, and inner strength.

To help snowy owls out over the colder months, avoid using rodenticides that can poison birds of prey, turn your garden into an owl hangout (it only takes five plants), and always give any owl you encounter plenty of space.

March babies are often associated with introspection, creativity, compassion and a thoughtful nature. It makes a whole lot of sense, then, that their birth month winter bird is the dark-eyed junco, a small but charming visitor that brings plenty of life to colder gardens.

Sometimes nicknamed the snowbird, dark-eyed juncos tend to spend much of their time quietly hopping around on the ground, searching for fallen seeds beneath trees and feeders. As such, they're a lovely match for March personalities: observant, gentle and happy to enjoy the quieter things in life.

If you fancy seeing more in your garden, remember that juncos are particularly fond of feeding on the ground, so provide a sheltered feeding area with small seeds. Leaving native grasses and seed heads standing also gives them more natural foraging opportunities.

If you're an April baby, you're thought to be charismatic, energetic, straightforward and naturally confident. As such, there's perhaps no better bird to represent you than the unmistakable blue jay.

With its brilliant blue plumage, distinctive crest and loud calls, the blue jay certainly knows how to make itself noticed. These intelligent members of the crow family have bold personalities that often make them one of the most entertaining birds to watch, in fact.

Blue jays are also clever problem-solvers, not to mention never afraid to make their presence known. Plant native oak trees and other nut-producing species to provide natural food and shelter to help them out, and remember that they also enjoy unsalted peanuts , so you can occasionally offer some in a suitable feeder.

I know, I know; a wild turkey might not feel like the most glamorous birth month winter bird, but hear me out! May personalities are often described as sociable, talented, loyal and strong-willed. And wild turkeys? Why, they're highly social, often gathering in flocks during winter as they search for food, not to mention putting on some impressive courtship displays.

They're also resourceful foragers, searching through woodland floors for seeds, nuts, berries and insects. During colder months, they can be spotted scratching through leaf litter and searching beneath trees for whatever food they can find. To help yours out, then, take care to leave natural cover in place where possible, and avoid using pesticides that reduce the insects and other natural food sources they rely on.

Known for its melancholy-sounding coo, the gentle mourning dove is a familiar sight across much of the United States, often seen perched on telephone wires, fences and tree branches, or quietly searching the ground for seeds.

They're also known for their strong pair bonds, with partners often working together to raise their young, so it's easy to see why this bird suits June babies (especially as they tend to be such romantic dreamers). It's a birth month winter bird that perfectly aligns with all of that sensitivity and imagination.

Remember, mourning doves enjoy feeding on the ground, so provide a clean feeding area with something like sunflower hearts if you want to see them in your garden. Plant some native seed-producing plants and shrubs, too, to help provide natural food and shelter.

July babies are adventurous free spirits known for their cheerful and (ahem) slightly slightly eccentric personalities, not to mention their strong protective streaks when it comes to family. It was inevitable, then, that the snow goose would prove their birth month winter bird.

Known for their impressive journeys between breeding and wintering grounds, snow geese are often spotted travelling in enormous flocks that fill the sky with their distinctive calls. They are also strongly connected to family life; young birds travel with their parents, and family groups can remain together during migration and winter.

If you want to help your feathery soulmate out, remember that snow geese depend on healthy wetlands, grasslands and agricultural landscapes during migration and winter. Supporting wetland conservation and protecting natural resting and feeding areas, then, is one of the best ways to keeo them thriving.

It makes so much sense that August babies get the delightful red-breasted nuthatch as their birth month winter bird, as it sums up all their best qualities perfectly. Yes, this little bird might be small, but it certainly doesn't lack confidence. One of its most entertaining habits is climbing headfirst down tree trunks, something it does with remarkable ease as it searches for insects and seeds hidden in the bark.

During autumn and winter, red-breasted nuthatches also collect and store conifer seeds, helping ensure they have food available when conditions become more challenging. They're hardworking, resourceful and rather determined, making them a lovely match for August birthdays.

To help them out, try to plant native trees such as pine, spruce and fir where appropriate for your region. You can also offer sunflower seeds, peanuts or suet in a clean feeder, particularly during colder weather.

Intelligent, spiritual, organized and analytical, September babies tend to be more successful in life (genuinely; look it up!) and thus are represented by the pine siskin.

These small finches might be easy to overlook at first, but they're fascinating little birds. They are adaptable, clever, and brimming with plenty of resourcefulness, though, which is why you often seen clinging to the tips of pine branches and even hanging upside down as they extract seeds from cones.

Sure, they're rather unpredictable visitors, as their movements depend heavily on food availability; this means that some gardens might welcome large flocks one winter and see very few the next. To keep them flocking, grow native conifers and other seed-producing plants to provide natural food, as well as nyjer (thistle) seed and sunflower hearts in finch feeders.

As an October baby myself, I'm more than happy to see this charming wanderer as my birth month winter bird! Especially as, with its striking yellow, black and white plumage and enormous pale bill, this chunky finch is one of North America's most distinctive birds.

Evening grosbeaks are adventurous travellers, sometimes moving considerable distances in search of food during autumn and winter. They're also wonderfully sociable, often gathering in groups around food sources and making quite an impression when they arrive at a backyard feeder.

For October birthdays, they're a lovely symbol of persistence, confidence and a little good fortune. After all, spotting one can feel like winning a small birdwatching lottery (although you can boost your chances with a handful of black-oil sunflower seeds, which can be offered in a sturdy platform feeder).

Empathetic, generous, brave and deeply determined, November-born souls are represented by one of North America's most magnificent waterbirds: the trumpeter swan.

These enormous white swans are impressive in every sense, with long necks, powerful wings and a distinctive black bill. They are known for their strong pair bonds and devoted parenting, and their family groups can be particularly striking to see gliding across a winter lake. In fact, it's their loyalty, protective instincts and graceful strength that make them a beautiful match for November birthdays.

These aren't traditional garden birds, so you can help them by protecting local wetlands, ponds and freshwater habitats, and always taking care to avoid disturbing swans when they're resting or feeding.

Generous, patriotic, optimistic and willing to take a few risks? Yeah, obviously the birth month winter bird for December is the cardinal!

Known for their strong pair bonds and the way they communicate through a variety of musical calls, their cheerful appearance against bare branches and snowy gardens has led to them becoming something not unlike a symbol of hope during the festive season. In fact, many believe that if they're the first bird you spot on January 1st, you're owed 12 months of love, optimism, and luck for the year ahead.

If you want to help yours out, plant native berry-producing shrubs such as winterberry, serviceberry and dogwood to make your garden a one-stop destination for food, shelter, and nesting opportunities.

Whether you believe in the concept of a birth month winter bird or not, there's no denying that they serve as a lovely reminder of just how much wildlife is around us, even during the coldest months of the year.

And perhaps the best part is that you don't have to wait for your own birth month to enjoy these feathered visitors. By planting native trees and shrubs, leaving seed heads standing, providing clean water and maintaining safe feeding areas, you can help make your garden a welcoming winter refuge for your feathered friends throughout the year.

Give it a go, I say! You'll do your bit for local wildlife and you might even discover a new favorite feathered friend along the way...

As such, her garden – designed around the stunning magnolia tree at its centre – is filled to the brim with pollinator-friendly blooms, homemade bird feeders, and old logs for insects to nest in.

Prediction: Where Will SpaceX Stock Be in 2030 Now That Flight 15 Has Launched?

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

SpaceX caught a Starship's upper stage for the first time but lost its booster. Three valuation scenarios show how the stock could fare by 2030.

By Rick Orford – Nov 21, 2026 at 6:30AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -7.69%) just reached another major milestone with its Starship program, and the outcome could have huge implications for where its stock heads by 2030. Specifically, Flight 15 launched Nov. 13, and SpaceX caught the upper stage the day after. However, the Super Heavy booster aborted its catch and was lost in the Gulf.

Overall, the catch could strengthen confidence in SpaceX's ambitions, while the lost booster could raise concerns around development timelines.

Here's what investors should watch and how the mixed result could affect SpaceX stock.

Starship is a heavy-lift launch vehicle designed to be fully reusable, enabling SpaceX to launch, recover, and fly its rockets again rather than building a new vehicle for every mission. Now that Starship's upper stage has been caught for the first time, the vehicle could be a future game changer for SpaceX as the company could change the economics of getting into space and back.

From there, if the company can make that process (reliably) repeatable, it could dramatically lower launch costs and increase the number of missions it can undertake. Plus, with a much larger payload capacity, Starship could potentially carry more cargo and satellites into orbit than existing rockets.

That could lead to cheaper launches and make previously uneconomical businesses more attractive, from satellite deployment and space-based communications to orbital infrastructure and lunar transportation.

Beyond that, Starship could also make it easier and cheaper for SpaceX to expand its Starlink business, allowing it to deploy more satellites, ramp up coverage, and increase network capacity.

Of course, the upper stage catch was only half of the planned double recovery. What does the lost booster mean for SpaceX?

SpaceX shares slipped about 0.6% on launch day, but have since edged higher. Even so, the lost booster could raise questions about development timelines, future launch costs, and the company's ability to deliver on its longer-term ambitions.

The unsuccessful booster recovery attempt could delay the timeline for achieving full reusability.

There's also a timing consideration. SpaceX has big ambitions for Starship, including its role in NASA's lunar program and the expansion of Starlink. Any delays would affect expectations surrounding both opportunities.

Still, the lost booster doesn't necessarily undermine the investment case for owning SpaceX. After all, Starship is a much more ambitious launch vehicle, so the development process is naturally more complicated. And if/when Starship finally becomes operational, it could provide the company with a new avenue for growth.

Today, SpaceX stock trades at a price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of about 61, and based on the latest reported sales data, that works out to $2.36 in sales per share. This high multiple may be justified, assuming revenue increases significantly as SpaceX successfully recovers Starship.

In a conservative case, if SpaceX generates $11.95 per share in sales, an 8-times compressed multiple could put shares near $96 by 2030, about 32% below today's level. At $17.49 per share in sales and a 12-times multiple, shares could trade near $210 by 2030, about 48% above today's level. Finally, in a more optimistic case, sales of $24.77 per share at 15 times sales could put shares near $372 by 2030, about 163% above today's level.

To be clear, these are scenarios, not promises, but they show where Starship's progress after Flight 15 could take the stock by 2030. In the end, the bigger opportunity is Starship's potential to make space transportation dramatically cheaper, opening up entirely new markets.

Watch: Milan loanee Fofana enjoying life at Sevilla as fans show their appreciation

Canva added an AI ‘kill switch’ to its tools

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Over the last few years, Canva, the Australian design platform, has been stuffing generative AI into its products. Now, it’s giving you the option to make those AI features disappear. On Thursday, the company announced two new switches to rein in its AI for anyone on Canva Free and Canva Pro. One toggle button hides its generative AI features. Another one will make Canva show less AI-generated material in its library of templates and graphics.

The first button seems like pure theater to me. If a user doesn’t want to use AI tools, it’s easy enough to figure out how: Don’t press the sparkly AI button. The second one is more useful, however, since it can help users avoid being drowned by AI-generated material while browsing the Canva libraries that made this suite popular in the first place. It’s worth noting that the switch doesn’t ban all AI-generated material from view—it just shows less.

Both switches are buried in two settings menus. The general AI “kill switch” lives under Profile > Settings > Permissions. Flip it off, and Canva’s generative features are “hidden across Canva,” the company said in an announcement. These include Magic Expand, which extends a photo, and Canva Craft, the first generative image model developed by the company. You can turn them back on whenever you like.

Still, Canva hasn’t erased every AI thingamajig. The suite’s AI-based Background Remover and Magic Grab—which picks an object out of a picture to move it somewhere else in the design—are “assistive editing tools,” and therefore are still accessed after toggling AI off.

The second control sits under Profile > Your Account > AI Personalization (more on this later). Turn it on, and Canva cuts back the AI-generated material that pops up in search results and recommendations, pushing more human-made stuff to the top. The company says its AI content is only “a small portion” of its library, like abstract backgrounds and illustrated graphics.

Why, really

The filter matters to people who don’t want to wade through a river full of generative AI creations. It also benefits the humans behind the designs. Canva pays actual humans each time someone uses their work in a design. In theory, that steers money toward the designers and illustrators who fill the Canva library.

These features are essentially a temperature check on AI. Canva tied the launch to the public mood, citing Pew Research Center figures: Roughly 6 in 10 Americans say they want more control over how artificial intelligence is used in their lives. “We believe the future of creativity should be defined by choice, not dictated by technology,” the company said in a statement.

In an email, Canva says it is “always listening to our community and looking for ways to make Canva work better for them. We’ve heard that people want more choice over AI, and these controls are one way we’re responding. . . . These controls give people more choice over how AI fits into their Canva experience.”

But if Canva really wanted to make this happen, why bury the controls? If you want to empower your users and creators, there’s no reason to hide the settings. Rather, you should add the switches right there on the main interface so that people can choose their experience with ease. The company says that putting clearly visible AI kill switches right on the main interface, for every user to see right away, is something they are “actually exploring.”

Perhaps Canva fears that too many people will choose to deactivate these features, cutting off one of its revenue streams. After all, in the same announcement, Canva said that its community has used its AI tools more than 38 billion times. The company also says it’s still investing to make its AI wares “even more powerful.” And remember: Canva just built its own generative image model to help shave the costs of so many people using its AI tools. AI is still very much a priority for the company.

Canva claims that “this isn’t a marketing play” and that it is just a temporary solution: “Our goal was to get a solution live to our community as quickly as possible, but that doesn’t mean this is its final form. While it’s early, the response is telling us that people are excited to have this option, but we’ll listen for signals on how it should evolve.”

Sony Bravia 9 II Review (2026): Adjustable and Bright

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

The current flagship television from Sony, the Bravia 9 II, is an RGB TV model (Sony calls it “True RGB”) that brings to life even the darkest scenes in Game of Thrones. Much brighter than the midrange Bravia 7 II, the 9 II's best qualities are related to its black levels, picture quality, and excellent viewing angles that allow me to see Jon Snow and the GoT dragons perfectly, even when I'm sitting off to the side.

This premium Sony television carries a premium price of $3,600 for the 65-inch model, but that’s still $1,400 less than the LG Micro RGB Evo, which retails for $5,000. I ended up preferring the overall picture quality of that LG model and some other OLED models, but—after my usual rigorous testing—I felt the Bravia 9 II came close to matching the high caliber of those other options. In fact, it’s just a notch or two lower than the absolute best television you might find.

The Sony Bravia 9 II is all black, sits on two feet, and includes four HDMI ports (only two are HDMI 2.1, though, which allows for higher-bandwidth streaming and sound at 48 Gbps). Setup was a breeze; Google TV was up and running in less than five minutes, which is an impressive feat, given that both Samsung and LG televisions typically add a few extra steps to the install process, such as authenticating your login (with Samsung) or relying on an app (with the LG ThinQ).

The premium Sony Bravia 9 II also offers exceptional sound. It includes two upfiring tweeters for more realistic Dolby Atmos than the Bravia 7 II, which lacks these extra channels. When I watched House of the Dragon, the room-filling audio made me feel like I was flying a dragon or piloting a ship.

Viewing angles on the Bravia 9 II are about the best I’ve seen on any television. I tested this element by camping out in a chair and playing video games off to the side, and there wasn’t a hint of screen glare. These perks are, again, a sign of its premium quality, verified through my usual benchmarks. Skin tones looked varied and vivid; one person had a little more red in her face than what I noticed on the LG Micro RGB Evo or the Samsung Micro RGB R95H.

In a demo-reel benchmark test I use for all televisions, I was shocked by the Bravia 9 II's stellar performance. I’ve watched the same short segment with a fence in front of a winter landscape countless times to test various models, but here, the green grass in the background looked supremely vivid and clear. It’s because the Bravia 9 II has such exceptional screen brightness, color accuracy, and absolutely no glare.

The Sony remote is just OK. I wish the Home button were centered; since it's not, I kept clicking the mic for the Google Gemini voicebot (which is in the center) by mistake. I prefer the bare-bones Samsung remote or the one included with an Apple TV 4K streaming device. Just give me a Netflix button and volume controls, and I’m good. Still, the layout of this remote mostly makes sense, and I like that it’s backlit.

The Bravia 9 II didn’t pass all of my tests for performance while I watched shows and movies, but it did allow me to tweak more settings than I’m used to. For example, the TV automatically calibrated colors and tones for Spider-Man: Brand New Day, but it still looked too dark. I was able to manually adjust the black level and used Vivid mode to make Spidey’s suit look extra red.

However, compared to the picture quality of an OLED like the Samsung S95H, the Bravia 9 II's deep blacks looked less realistic. That's because on OLED TVs, the LEDs can be turned off entirely. The Bravia 9 II performed OK for Toy Story 5, but in darker scenes late in the film, I noticed the Samsung had inky blacks that reminded me of a high-end projector.

My main takeaway is that it's nice that you can tweak settings on the Bravia 9 II to great effect, thanks to an RGB panel that’s super-bright. The movies Awake and The Creator tested my patience a little in that regard. I adjusted the black level to precisely 61 (out of 100) and could then see faces, a predawn mountain scene, and a beach landing with better clarity. Knowing you can achieve those results is nice; actually doing it does take time.

While playing Resident Evil: Requiem to test brightness, picture quality, and latency, I confirmed that it's possible to adjust the black level to liven up dark scenes. I also noticed that the latency, at a 120-Hz refresh rate when I used one of the HDMI 2.1 ports, was almost nonexistent. That helped with every slight twitch when a seven-headed monster from the dark underworld of hell started attacking me.

Similar games, like Senua's Saga: Hellblade II, looked too gray and washed out on the midrange Bravia 7 II and some LED models I’ve tested. I also played End of Abyss, a game that takes place almost entirely in dark hallways, and the Bravia 9 II rendered those scenes with inky blacks—granted, not as black as an OLED would, but better than many LED TVs.

Bravia 9 II lived up to its flagship-quality promise for sportscasts, making my beloved Minnesota Vikings' purple jerseys look vivid and clear and the grass bright green—all without displaying any glare at all. News programs were bright, with strong color accuracy, but not oversaturated—a good compromise. In fact, it was so clear, I felt as if I could reach out to clip an MS Now analyst's nose hairs.

The Bravia 9 II is near the top of the leaderboard of the best televisions to buy right now. You'll just want to consider the technology it uses. RGB models use red, green, and blue LEDs to create realistic colors that, in theory, improve color accuracy and vividness. Unlike an OLED TV, an RGB TV still shines those LED colors through an LCD panel. So you have to pick your poison. The Bravia 9 II looked super bright and vivid, especially during NFL games. It’s just a hair less colorful and vivid compared to the LG Micro RGB Evo.

Knowing you can tweak the settings for the results you want and that anyone in your household will be able to see America’s Got Talent from any angle in the room is a major plus. But is the Sony Bravia 9 II worth $3,600 when so many TVs cost around $2,000? I would say it depends on what you watch. I want to see dragons at night and monsters in dark hallways, but for some animated shows, sports, and network TV, you might just want your device to work well, right out of the box, without worrying about cinematic quality. Yes, a flagship lets you do a lot more, but at a much steeper price.

Marco Materazzi’s disturbing parallel between Zidane’s headbutt and the death of Jesus de Miguel

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Twenty years after the 2006 World Cup final, Marco Materazzi has still not turned the page on his altercation with Zinédine Zidane. A few hours before the clash between Italy and France, this Thursday (8:45 p.m.) in the League of Nations, the former Italian defender returned to the famous headbutt received in the chest, drawing a parallel with the recent death of Jesus de Miguel. The Tenerife striker died on November 3 after a violent collision with opposing goalkeeper Jesus Owono. A tragedy which pushed Materazzi to wonder about the consequences that Zidane’s gesture in 2006 could have had. The death of Jesus de Miguel made me thank God that nothing serious happened. In the case of this goalkeeper, we are talking about involuntary homicide following a game action… What if that had happened to me? “, he confided to La Gazzetta dello Sport. A surprising comparison, as the circumstances of the two incidents are different. During the 2006 World Cup final, Zidane deliberately hit Materazzi after a verbal provocation from the Italian, causing his exclusion before the Nazionale’s victory on penalties. Last October, the former number 10 of the Blues returned to this episode by admitting that he was not proud of his gesture, without directly apologizing to his former opponent.

A position that Marco Materazzi continues to regret. “ In his place, I would have said: “I made a mistake, but I was provoked. Judge for yourselves what happened, I apologize.” He would have passed for a great lord, and Materazzi for the usual moron “, explained the former Inter Milan player. The Italian world champion also recounted an unexpected meeting with Zidane in November 2010, in Milan, on the eve of a match between AC Milan and Real Madrid. According to his account, he spontaneously went to shake hands with the Frenchman, who replied: “ Don’t worry, these things happen, it’s in the past. » Materazzi, however, regrets that Zidane then claimed to have taken him for a simple supporter. Despite this persistent dispute, the Italian does not hide his admiration for the coaching qualities of the former playmaker. I like everything about his approach. He knows the players, understands the champions, gives them space and creates empathy. It’s fundamental to winning “, he stressed. He particularly praised his ability to manage the big personalities of the Real Madrid locker room, citing Cristiano Ronaldo, Gareth Bale, Marcelo and Luka Modric, with whom Zidane won numerous trophies.

Binance Futures Volume Hits Two-Month High Amid Prolonged Market Volatility

📅 2026-11-21  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The world’s largest cryptocurrency exchange by volume, Binance is witnessing a rapid surge in its futures trading activity as the broader crypto market continues to face increased selling pressure.

While the market is still struggling with volatility and rapidly declining investor interest, the latest on-chain data from the crypto analytics platform CryptoQuant shows that futures traders have become extremely active on Binance.

The data shows that both Bitcoin and Ethereum have seen their perpetual futures trading volume on Binance surge to a combined high of $39.8 billion as of November 20.

Notably, this marks the highest level the metric has reached since September 21, indicating that traders are actively adjusting their positions to hedge against further losses as volatility continues to intensify.

Nonetheless, Bitcoin saw the highest increase in perpetual futures volume as it surged to $24.75 billion on the same day.

The data further showed that the volume was slightly higher than the $24.57 billion recorded on September 21, when Bitcoin was trading above $87,000.

Ethereum, on the other hand, followed with a notable increase in the same metric, recording $15.05 billion in perpetual futures volume on the same day.

However, Ethereum did not entirely mimic Bitcoin in the trend as this was still below the $17.27 billion recorded during the September 21 trading period.

Further data shows that the high futures trading activity recorded on Binance on November 20 has coincided with BlackRock’s record Bitcoin ETF outflow.

The data shows that BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded $511 million in net outflows on that day, marking a sharp reversal from the positive inflows seen in September.

While this shows that investors are also pulling their funds from Bitcoin ETFs, it marked the highest single-day outflow BlackRock has ever recorded.

Wusongkou Forum Unveils River-Sea Cruise Plan and Consumption Index

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The 2026 Wusongkou Forum took place on Nov. 8 at the Shanghai International Cruise Tourism Resort in Baoshan. It brought together regulators, port authorities, cruise operators and industry experts to discuss what organizers called “three-cruise synergy,” which is the coordinated development of ocean cruise ships, river cruise vessels and yachts.

Hosted by Shanghai Wusongkou Culture & Tourism Investment (Group) Co., the event included the launch of a “Three-Cruise Synergy · A New Voyage from River to Sea” program. Four initiatives were announced.

The first is a “Cruise Plus” brand renewal plan. It targets four consumer segments: seniors, weddings and romance travel, sporting events and younger travelers. The goal is to turn passenger volume into more onboard and shoreside spending.

The second is a river-sea linkage project that uses Wusongkou’s location at the mouth of the Yangtze to connect inland river cities with ocean itineraries. Its flagship product is the Yangtze No. 3, which has begun sailing between Chongqing and Shanghai from the Wusongkou International Cruise Terminal. It is the first river-sea cruise service to cover the full length of the Yangtze.

Third, China Huaxia International Cruise Co. released October 2026 data for its China Cruise Consumption Vitality Index. The company described it as the country’s first regular data tool for the cruise market.

The fourth initiative links the cruise terminal with Shanghai Baoshan railway station, which is due to open next year as the meeting point of the Shanghai–Chongqing–Chengdu high-speed line and the Shanghai–Nantong railway. Plans include combined rail and cruise ticketing, a direct shuttle, baggage transfer from car door to cabin door and shared VIP benefits.

On forum day, the Adora Magic City and the Adora Flora City, China’s first two domestically built large cruise ships, were berthed together at Wusongkou for the first time.

Goalkeeper involved in fatal collision with Spanish star Jesus de Miguel, 30, breaks cover for the first time after tragic accident to pay tribute to Tenerife player

📅 2026-11-21  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Published: 06:20 EST, 21 November 2026 | Updated: 06:30 EST, 21 November 2026

The goalkeeper involved in the collision that killed Spanish footballer Jesus de Miguel has been seen in public for the first time since the tragic accident.

During a match earlier this month, Andorra keeper Jesus Owono came flying out of his goal and wiped out Tenerife star de Miguel with a high challenge which resulted in the striker being admitted to intensive care, where he later died.

De Miguel's passing shocked the footballing world and Owono was subsequently given a 12-match ban by authorities.

Both sides have been back in action since the tragedy, but on Friday, Andorra hosted Leganes at their Estadi de la FAF.

That was their first home game since de Miguel's passing, and they made sure to pay a moving tribute to him on the night.

Before the match, the players went onto the pitch wearing shirts with his name and number on them and they carried out a bouquet of white flowers.

Andorra keeper Jesus Owono was seen in public for the first time since Jesus de Miguel's tragic death as he attended a tribute to the former Tenerife star

Before Andorra's game with Legannes, several tributes were paid, including a minute's silence

A minute's silence was also held before the game, while those in attendance applauded during the ninth minute of the match, the shirt number worn by de Miguel at Tenerife.

The most striking part of the evening though was Owono's attendance in the stands.

The goalkeeper has stayed away from the public eye since de Miguel's death, after which he was subjected to chants of 'murderer', but he wanted to attend the tribute at his club's home ground.

Owono was wearing a black coat and hat as he stood next to team-mates who weren't involved in the matchday squad.

Despite receiving the support of the crowd and his team-mates, local reports said Owono cut a downcast figure and that he was seen praying immediately after the minute's silence.

Owono's desire to attend the tribute came after he had also asked to meet de Miguel's family, something he hopes to do one day.

Meanwhile, Tenerife returned to action last week against AD Ceuta CF in a match that was also filled with tributes.

Players provided a floral tribute as they arrived for the match, and gathered outside gate nine of the Heliodoro Rodríguez Lopez Stadium – another nod to de Miguel's shirt number.

Grief-stricken Tenerife stars then took to the field carrying a bouquet of flowers, and placed it at the scene of the tragedy.

Both sets of players and fans then observed a minute's silence in memory of de Miguel before the game kicked off.

The game was then brought to a pause in the ninth minute, so the players could pay another tribute.

Fans inside the stadium unveiled a tifo which read: 'Jesus de Miguel, 9, eternal blanquiazul', which translates to: 'Jesus de Miguel, 9, eternally white and blue.'

Other fans held up white and blue cards beside the tifo, with others in the ground applauding. When Tenerife scored, de Miguel's shirt was held in the air.

After the game, which Tenerife won 2-0, both teams gathered in the centre circle to place de Miguel's shirt on a bouquet of flowers, and the fans applauded to bring an end to an emotional day.

Owono was watching the game from the stands as he stood next to some of his team-mates

Tenerife returned to action last week and paid several tributes to their former star de Miguel

The tragedy occurred as the game between Tenerife and Andorra entered the seventh minute of stoppage time, when de Miguel looked set to race away on the counter-attack for his side in search of a winner.

As he watched a long ball drop into his path, the striker was struck in the chest by Owono's flying knee as he attempted to clear the ball near the halfway line.

After initially rolling around on the floor, de Miguel became motionless, and medical staff rushed onto the pitch to attend to him.

Owono was given a red card for the challenge before the decision was soon taken by the referee to abandon the fixture.

Owono's 12-match ban is currently in effect, yet his manager Carles Manso hinted that he may never play again.

He said: 'We will ​give him space so that he feels completely free to make ‌any ⁠decision, whether he wants to return, when, or if he does not want to return.

'When he feels it is appropriate and when ​he feels genuinely ready to take a ​decision ⁠like that (he will do so).'

Harry Kane was withdrawn after 20 minutes of Bayern's Bundesliga game against Cologne; the injury comes days after the England captain became his country's most-capped player; Kane won the Ballon d'Or earlier this month; Michael Olise also taken off injured in 1-1 draw

Please use Chrome browser for a more accessible video player

Harry Kane is targeting the "business end of the season" after undergoing surgery on a torn ligament in his left ankle.

Kane wrote on social media: "The highs and lows of life! Obviously very disappointed to get this injury but as always it's an opportunity to show resilience and improvement going into 2027.

"I'll be back fresh and ready to go for the business end of the season!

"Tough times don't last, tough people do" - my guy @edelman11 (Julian Edelman, three-time Super Bowl winner with the New England Patriots) told me this one!

Kane - who became England's most-capped player last week - sustained the injury when he was scythed down in the area by Cologne defender Joel Schmied, who reached Michael Olise's low cross first but clattered into the Ballon d'Or winner's left leg in his follow-through at the back post.

He went into the international break having a drawn a blank in his four previous matches for Bayern but he scored twice for England, including from the spot in their 3-2 Nations League win over Spain.

But after being forced off after 20 minutes at the Allianz Arena, replaced by Tom Bischof, Kane could now be facing a battle to be fully fit for England's two-legged quarter-final against Norway in March.

A three-month period on the sidelines would rule Kane out for 11 Bundesliga games, the DFB-Pokal round of 16 tie against Hertha Berlin, as well as all four of Bayern's remaining Champions League league phase matches.

Providing his recovery goes to plan, that would give the striker just over a month to prepare for England's Nations League quarter-final.

Hailey Bieber’s It-Jacket Wardrobe Is Becoming Untenable

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Hailey Bieber must have some serious wardrobe storage. The Rhode founder has long been a devoted collector of excellent outerwear—oversized leather bombers, vintage furs, sculptural shearling, and the occasional extravagant Conner Ives robe—but her latest run of looks in New York has taken things to another level. At this point, we’re struggling to keep up with her ever-expanding collection of It-jackets.

The last few days have seen Bieber leave just about everybody else out in the cold. On November 17, she stepped out a Phoebe Philo shaggy Dancer jacket: cropped, voluminous, in mottled brown and gray shearling. She paired it with her trusty light-wash Levi’s Ribcage jeans, vintage black Manolo Blahnik sandals, and amber-tinted Gucci sunglasses. The jacket, now sold out, was the undisputed star of the outfit. It’s a tried-and-true Bieber styling trick and very much a formula to follow this season.

The following day, she dipped into Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy’s wardrobe playbook for an appearance at the New York Times DealBook Summit. Bieber wore a black Yohji Yamamoto jacket from spring 1998, paired with a white pencil skirt and black pumps. The same design was famously worn by Bessette-Kennedyin 1998. It was a major archival find.

But she wasn’t finished. Later, Bieber emerged in an ivory satin opera coat by New York-based Oriori Maison, framed by a dramatic swathe of voluminous gray-white fur, worn with black trousers, dark sunglasses, and her trusty Inez pumps from Reformation.

The designer behind the coat, Xiaoxi Zhang, tells Vogue that Bieber’s stylist contacted her directly to arrange the pull. For Zhang, whose designs explore Chinese heritage through a contemporary wardrobe, the moment is particularly meaningful. “I’ve always loved my cultural heritage, but I’ve never really worn traditional Chinese clothing,” Zhang shared. “Before making my first Chinese-inspired jacket, my intention was always to find a way to bring elements of Chinese culture into everyday fashion—to make heritage feel natural, modern, and wearable.”

Zhang made her first jacket in 2024, initially just for herself, rather than as a business venture. Discovering vintage quilts helped crystallize her approach. “It was the beginning of seeing my culture through my own perspective, rather than simply recreating the traditional,” she says. Her luxurious satin-and-fur creation has now found its way onto one of fashion’s most closely watched dressers. Her one-of-one pieces using sumptuous archival textiles are dropped via Instagram, if you want to emulate this excellent look.

By November 19, Bieber was back in black leather, arriving at Rigor Hill Market in a glossy, longline leather coat, relaxed trousers, and black shoes worn with white socks.

Of course, Bieber’s expansive love of leather outerwear is nothing new. Earlier this fall, she was spotted in a fringed leather blazer, while her collection of oversized bombers and sharply tailored jackets has long been the foundation of her off-duty wardrobe. What’s changed is the sheer variety: from minimalist archival tailoring to flamboyant, fur-trimmed opera coats, Bieber is evidently having fun with her outerwear, heralding a post-quiet luxury era that keeps things cozy.

And she’s certainly not alone. As Vogue recently observed, the personality coat is having a major moment, with Jennifer Lawrence embracing Colleen Allen’s chartreuse velvet housecoat, and other fashion favorites turning to extravagant silhouettes and trimmings.

If there’s one thing we’ve learned from Hailey Bieber’s fall wardrobe, it’s that you can never have too many great jackets. Though, at this rate, she may need another home to house them all.

‘Project Hail Mary’ Almost Had a Completely Different Ending for Rocky

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In a historic move, actor and puppeteer James Ortiz is eligible for a Best Supporting Actor nomination at the 99th Academy Awards for his standout performance on Project Hail Mary. He’s the mind and movement behind the blockbuster’s lovable alien creature, Rocky, who led a team of puppeteers on set, essentially performing “Waiting for Godot in space” opposite Ryan Gosling with take after take of improvisation.

From filmmakers Phil Lord and Chris Miller, Project Hail Mary took audiences out of this world in an Andy Weir adaptation, penned by Drew Goddard, that wasn’t just visually stunning, but was a surprisingly moving two-hander between a man way in over his head and a creature made of rock, both on a dangerous mission to save their home planets.

At this year’s SCAD Savannah Film Festival, Collider’s Steve Weintraub had the pleasure of moderating an in-depth Q&A with Ortiz to discuss the artistry of bringing one of 2026’s most memorable characters to life. Alongside Gosling’s Ryland Grace, Rocky becomes Project Hail Mary’s beating heart, and during this conversation, Ortiz shares the behind-the-scenes work it took to bring them from the page to the screen. We learn all about the practical puppetry, the limitations that allowed their creativity to soar, how Rocky’s most iconic moments and quips came to be. Don't miss the full Q&A in the video above, with a transcript provided below.

COLLIDER: Let's talk a little bit about your background and how you got involved in the project.

JAMES ORTIZ: So I was cast as an actor a thousand years ago by the casting director, Jeanne McCarthy. You may have seen her name at the end of this film. I was cast in a pilot that no one ever saw because it never aired. It was for a network that we don't have to mention.

It was a great experience, but shortly after that, in New York, where I'm based, I was developing this sort of side career that turned into my full career, which is puppeteering and designing puppets for the theater. It kind of became this funny opportunity for me to play characters against my type. I could play things that I would never, ever be cast as because not every day you need, like, I don't know, a gay mad scientist. So, sometimes you need other flavors, and it was so exciting to be able to do that.

So Jeanne remembered that I existed, and she would bring me in for stuff, but oftentimes I would go, "Oh, I can't come in because I have this puppet thing." And she would go, "Puppets?" And long story short, she gave me a phone call. She told me about this story, and I went, "This sounds incredible, but do they really mean a puppet? Do they mean that?" And I had a great meeting with Phil [Lord] and Chris [Miller]. It was love at first sight. We absolutely adore each other. And then we were off to the races, and I was on set trying to make a faceless five-legged creature lovable.

If Rocky could watch Project Hail Mary, what would he complain the filmmakers got wrong about him?

ORTIZ: I think Rocky doesn't think that they're funny, so I think Rocky would hear everybody laughing and go, "Not funny, not funny." I think that'd probably be the biggest thing.

If you could sit down with Jim Henson, what would you want to talk about?

ORTIZ: I don't know if I would say anything. I would just sort of sit there like a loser. I've been really fortunate; I've met a lot of his children because they run different puppet organizations all over the country, and it's been really, really special. I did a show called The Woodsman a thousand years ago in New York, and it was really, really special. Cheryl Henson moderated a talk-back very much like this, and she told me very effusively how much her father loved the Oz stories, and it was really meaningful to hear that.

How many voicemails have you recorded for friends and family as Rocky?

ORTIZ: The limit does not exist. I couldn't even begin to give you a number.

How do you feel about doing the voice in public, like in moments like this?

ORTIZ: Yeah. What are we trying to communicate?

I'm sure there are some people in the audience that would like a voicemail from Rocky.

ORTIZ: I think what it would probably be would be, "You reach voicemail of this human. Make no sense. Leave message after beep. Beep now. It's time for a beep. Where is beep, question? Make no sense. Make no sense. Uh, uh, uh." And then it gets cut off, because I don't think Rocky fully understands what's happening.

When was the first time you said, "Fist my bump," and did you realize what it was going to become?

ORTIZ: I had no idea. We worked on the movie. We worked so hard, and I left, and I had such a great experience. And then I really had no sense that anybody would like it, frankly. That's maybe a failing of mine, but I've been around long enough to know that even if everybody that worked on it did everything right, it doesn't always translate. It doesn't always land, and the fact that it has is pretty, pretty wild. So, I didn't really think that far ahead, to be honest.

Frankly, also, I was only meant to be the person on set to help Ryan [Gosling], to help the DP. I was the steward of the character. I was just borrowing Rocky. And the fact that they lent him fully to me towards the end is still something I'm going to be processing for the rest of my life.

I'm going to dig down a little bit, but do you remember when you first said, "Fist my bump?"

ORTIZ: It had to have been in one of the scenes. It probably was in an improv that we were just kind of going. I was working so hard on just trying to make Rocky as truthful as possible that sometimes I would forget that there were catchphrases I had to hit. So most of the way that we were shooting is that Phil and Chris had an earpiece in my ear, and they could offer thoughts in between, in takes. So sometimes they would go, "Say fist my bump," and you're like, "Right. ‘Amaze, amaze, amaze,’” or whatever, right? So I can't actually tell you when I did it, but I know it would've been at some point then.

You said you and Ryan would sometimes improvise for incredibly long stretches. What's something Ryan did or said as Grace that completely caught you off guard, and how did you respond as Rocky?

ORTIZ: [Laughs] The only thing that's coming to mind right now is because when we shot it, remember that meme that went around three years ago that was “very demure?” There was one day where we were just going and riffing and riffing and going, and at some point, we were talking about Rocky or something, and he went, "It's very demure." And you saw him in real-time go, "No,” and try to backpedal. And in real-time, I think I went, "What is demure, question?"

We just kind of would go. We would just waste camera time.

We're at SCAD, and there are a lot of students in the audience, and I am 100% sure there are a few people out there that would love to do what you do. What advice would you like to share? And part two is, what advice were you given that was absolute horseshit?

ORTIZ: I may have to curse to answer this question, too. I was told by an acting professor a long time ago, "This is one motherfucker of a business." And, yes, it's hard, but I don't think that means it's bad. I think the most rewarding things have been the things that have been hard-won. That's what I would say.

But I would also say about the first question, if you want to play characters that are not easy to define, act more. Act all the time. Try to get opportunities to play characters that you wouldn't be cast as. Read roles that you're like, "I don't think this is for me. Let's try." And also, I think puppetry is an inherently physical art form, so I would also say invest in using your body to play the character.

Something that I've been saying endlessly all over this process has been saying that Rocky's movement is actually Rocky's subtext. The thing that Rocky is feeling is actually what is coming out in his body. The thing that he's saying is the best the translator can manage. So, that was something that I was always tracking: What is Rocky feeling at any given moment? Even though I don't know if Rocky has a deep sense of that themself. They don't. And over the course of the story, we get to see that Rocky actually learns what feeling is through Grace. So, that was another whole evolution.

But I guess the answer is movement theater, playing wacky parts, and letting your imagination not be limited.

I would also say, just from my perspective, I don't think people realize you really have to work your ass off to do something. You've got to give almost everything of yourself to something that you love to be successful.

ORTIZ: Yeah. I think that's completely right. I knew when I got the job that it wasn't going to be me at the end, but what does that matter? My job was to be of service. My job was to make the film work. My job was to make this character that is totally untenable — five legs, no face, speaks in this way, comes from a culture that is unimaginable — and I just went, "I want to always deliver something that feels consistent, that feels alive, that feels surprising, and I want to help Greig Fraser, who's shooting this movie, who has to emotionally find where the camera should go, and emotionally help Ryan in these long scenes where he has so much to do." Can you imagine him trying to do this to a tennis ball? It would've been a nightmare.

So, it was always the objective of just going all the way, and I think I have a “too much” gene as it is, so I couldn't help it.

Was there a moment during filming when you realized Rocky had stopped being a puppet and had become a real character in everyone's eyes?

ORTIZ: Everybody was talking to Rocky and not me. [Laughs] It was exactly that.

The first few weeks of the process, we were still finding our sea legs and figuring some stuff out. Phil gave me some great advice. We shot a scene that actually didn't make the cut — it's on the deleted scenes. My first day of shooting was this scene where we were in these two bunk beds. It was a great scene, and I loved doing it, but you could feel everybody on set who might've been skeptical that this would work, you could feel every crew member's shoulders just relax and lean in.

But Phil pulled me into his little tent with all my other Rocketeers, and we looked at some of the footage from that day, and he was like, "I think you can trust stillness more," because I was overcomplicating the fact that he doesn't have a face means you have to communicate through movement. But I think I was doing too much, and he was like, "Remember, this is in IMAX, so you can't be like [thrashes around] the entire time.”

So, it was a really cool lesson. It was a little bit like going to the Michael Caine school of acting, of never blink, always look at that side of the camera, not that. It was all that stuff that I was learning on a creature with no face.

By the way, that advice that Michael Caine said about not blinking is dead-accurate.

If any of you are acting students, I swear to you, you've got to learn how to not blink, and if you look at people's performances, like great actors, you will notice they do not blink. They're very specific when they blink because they feel that it's like a character moment.

I swear it's a little thing that you should absolutely practice.

ORTIZ: There's footage you can find of Michael Caine explaining this. Do it. It's so valuable because it's about using the medium. Theater people know how to use their whole body, and a lot of film acting is like, "This is now your proscenium. What are you going to do? How do you use it?" And, yeah, that's always the exercise.

Let's talk a little bit about what the physical rig was like. How many people does it take to operate Rocky, and how do you keep the team in sync?

ORTIZ: Great question. So it was me — I was basically always on the carapace, the main body. There were handles coming out of his mid-back, sort of like a fighter pilot, and that was how I could animate the movement of the head. There were sometimes three to seven people operating his other appendages, and I was always sort of leading the charge on what that was.

So we spent eight weeks basically learning how to work together as kind of a company. But the thing that would keep us tethered together, because puppetry can inherently be very technical — I promised Ryan the first day that we started talking about this project, and I had already gotten the part, I did say, "Listen, puppetry can be technical, but I guarantee you can improv with me, and I'm not going to leave you hanging, and I'm not going to let the technical side win." Right? It has to be an emotional performance.

And sometimes when I could feel my other performers trying to do one thing with a leg, and it wasn't going to happen, and they were getting frustrated, all of that, I would just say, "Let's not try to aim for perfection. Let's just focus on the feeling." So sometimes we would finish a take, and I would go, "That was awesome. Let's try it again, and this time it's Christmas morning, and you're tiptoeing down the stairs, and you're so excited." That has nothing to do with the scene, but that's the emotional beat that we were trying to sort of land.

So, that would be the one way, especially when we would be improvising and had to just lean in.

Was there ever a scene where the limitations of the puppet made the performance better?

ORTIZ: [Thumbs down] So, I think it's been well documented that basically they were like, "So he's going to do a thumbs up," and I was like [shakes head], because Rocky's elbows don't rotate. Rocky doesn't have wrists, which was something I would say all the time, and they'd go, "What does that mean?" And I was like, "Well, you can't do a thumbs up without a wrist." And we would try to do his finger, but that looked like something else. That looked like, "One minute!" So that didn't work.

That was something that we sort of showed on the day, and it was something that one of my puppeteers was like, "Wouldn't it be funny if he goes, 'Thumbs up?'" [Thumbs down] And it is! So that was one sort of happy accident, as Bob Ross would say.

Phil and Chris are known for finding movies in the editing room. Did they do anything with the editing that really surprised you about Rocky's performance?

ORTIZ: Yes. What I loved about working with them is that, again, I was a theater person from nowhere. The theater credits don't always transfer in the world of movies, and they would shoot every scene until basically everybody's ideas had been wrung out. It wasn't just like, "Let's do the scene on paper." Which, by the way, Drew Goddard wrote an incredible script, and we would do that, and then Ryan would have a cool idea, or I would have a cool idea, or Phil and Chris would go, "We were thinking it would be fun if..." And we would just shoot. Sometimes it would be a three-line scene, and we would shoot a take for 45 minutes, going back and going back, and, “Try it again, and add this.”

So, we had so much material that frankly, I left every day going, "I have no idea what we just did." I completely blacked out. I was just trying to stay in the scene and trying to stay alive. So when I saw different cuts of the movie, they in fact were very different films from my estimation. The way that the scenes would be juxtaposed next to each other meant something else.

When I saw the final cut, I actually did go, "I think I want to try to get into the ADR booth and maybe try to complete some of Rocky's arcs a little bit more." Because we didn't have the real estate. The movie is from Ryan's perspective. Literally, you're inside of his head most of the time. When he realizes that Rocky speaks that way, we suddenly can see in Rocky Vision, right? So there wasn't a lot of real estate to put Rocky's arc in there.

The TLDR of what I'll just sort of say here is that we didn't have this moment where Rocky's sort of traumatic 80 years left alone on a spaceship was ever paid off. Through a series of just trial and error, we were able to find something, and I sort of raised my hand and said, "I think we need to change this line in this scene where I give him the astrophage.” And Rocky saying, "Grace say Grace will die, Rocky fix" was something that we added truly a month before the movie came out. And it was something that really made me so happy because, again, it's that moment when I was going, "I feel so much for this character. I really want to do him service." And we hadn't landed the plane on, well, where does that trauma go? We never addressed it. And I think the trauma has to turn into something positive. Rocky then takes the awful experience that they had, which we don't really talk about, and turns it into help for the person that he cares about the most.

So anyway, that's a roundabout way of answering that.

It has to be really crazy to be a part of a film like this that means so much to so many people. What has it been like meeting fans, and what do they always want to talk to you about?

ORTIZ: Oh, it's incredible. Like I was saying, we made this movie kind of in a bubble, and then it came out — and what we don't have time for is the time that I met Steve the first time. Do you want to do it? Can we do it?

This is your Q&A. You do whatever you want.

ORTIZ: Let's do this because I think it's real charming. So basically, we were doing a press junket in LA before the movie came out, and the only people that had seen the movie were press. We were doing this thing where they were being interviewed downstairs, and I was up with the Rocketeers doing basically a meet-and-greet with Rocky. So press was coming in. There were little bits of footage. The footage was still coming out. And it was good, but it was quick. It was two minutes with each person.

We should say the room that you were in was in a hotel, and there were curtains, so you could not see past the curtain to know what was inside. And the publicist would only say, "We have something we want you to do." We were not told what was inside that room. At least for me, they were like, "We just want you to go in the room."

ORTIZ: And basically, there were cameras set up that people could see Rocky because Rocky was on a table and alive, and, "Hello, friend!" And we would interact, and it would be a two-minute sit in the chair, take a picture, have a video, move on. And it had become kind of a little rote, and everybody was like, "I loved the movie," and that is kind of a kiss of death. We're like, "Did you?"

And this human walked in, the curtain parted, and Steve's face went [looks concerned]. And we were trying to engage, like, "Come over here and take a picture." He went [holds up hand and shakes head], and he just sort of moved towards us, and it was so moving. I told you this already. It was so moving because it was the first inkling I had that maybe this character worked because you were so affected. I had to break character. I was still puppeteering, and I went, "Steve, do you want to sit down?" It was so moving. We went long just to let you have your experience. It was really meaningful to me.

I'm very, very lucky with what I get to do, and as I told you backstage, I've interviewed Kermit and Miss Piggy. Literally, you just become a child. You do not see the puppeteers. You just see Kermit and Miss Piggy, and you just want to talk to them, and you completely forget about everybody else. I was just in London for something that I can't talk about, but they had, in this one room, the Rocky puppets. I was just there seeing them, and everyone was freaking out.

ORTIZ: That’s cool. That's nice to hear. Anyway, non-sequitur. I wanted to tell you that story because we had met before, and it was really meaningful to me. It was really meaningful, Steve.

Is there a deleted Rocky scene or an extended sequence that you think fans would absolutely lose their minds over if they saw?

ORTIZ: There's one little moment, and it wasn't even a deleted scene; it was sort of an addition to a scene. There's a scene when we are training to go fishing, and the actress who played the voice of Mary, Priya [Kansara], who's an incredible actress and was on set with us for only a few days. It was thrilling because Ryan and I had been shooting for five months, just me and him, basically doing Waiting for Godot in space, and then Priya would show up, and we had another scene partner, so we all just woke up in a fun way.

We had this funny idea that was maybe just for one or two takes total, where when Grace was sleeping, Mary and Rocky had been hanging out, and that Mary had taught Rocky knock-knock jokes. So in the middle of the scene, she goes, "Tell joke now." And then Rocky went, "Knock, knock." And then Grace went, "Ugh, who's there?" "Rocky!" And that was it. It went nowhere, and it didn't help anything, but it was a moment on set where we all kind of went, "Oh, this is silly. We're having a silly time."

What was the single most technically difficult Rocky scene to perform, and what's something audiences would never realize went into making those few seconds work?

ORTIZ: God, I don't know if there's one scene I could say that was really the hardest thing ever. What I would say is… I'll give you some wacky fun facts. So, every scene was a total challenge. Anytime that you see Rocky in camera, we were really there putting him there. The challenging part was making sure that we had enough space for the puppeteers, not Rocky, in every single take — and I mean shot, not scene. Every single shot there's a different use of it's CG, it's half CG, it's fully puppet, it's two-part puppets, whatever.

But I will say the scene where we're playing with the tape measure, the thing that thrills me beyond belief is that there's only one shot or two shots total in that when it gets cut back and forth that is CG. Everything else is us. I have to shout out Claire Roi Harvey, one of my puppeteers, and Fred [Buhagiar]. They were literally using these mechanical fingers to do this thing, and we would just cut and play, and it was the silliest day on set. It was so fun.

The hardest thing was the fact that they made Rocky out of fiberglass, which is what I asked for. It's a lightweight material. But all the Xenonite is made out of plexiglass. If you don't know, fiberglass will scratch the hell out of plexiglass, and you can't polish it. It's a streak, and it's ruined. So, we weren't allowed to touch our house because every time that we did, it would leave a God-awful gash, and then Greig didn't have anything to shoot through. So, they basically added layers of transparency the first few days that was protectant, but it made it more blurry, and then Greig was not happy, and then we had to go back and forth.

So there's only two shots in the movie — it's actually in the clock when Rocky pulls out the clock — that there's real glass that's actually there because real glass doesn't scratch the way that it does with fiberglass. So everything else is this bizarre thing where we weren't allowed to touch our home, which is challenging, as you can imagine.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

If AI Chips Become Commoditized, Nvidia Has the Most to Lose, While These 3 Stocks Are Set to Gain

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia has a lot to lose as AI chips become fungible, while these stocks could gain.

By Geoffrey Seiler – Nov 21, 2026 at 6:15AM EST

The AI chip market is starting to shift, and with it, Nvidia's (NVDA -14.01%) grip. Anthropic recently revealed that its Claude Mythos model could start running part of its AI models on alternative AI chips to Nvidia's graphics processing units (GPUs) in just a few days with basically no human intervention, a process that used to take six to 12 months and dozens of engineers. This greatly reduces Nvidia's CUDA moat at a time when competitors are starting to catch up.

Alphabet's (GOOGL -2.70%) (GOOG -2.59%) Tensor Processing Units (TPUs) have started to outperform Nvidia's GPUs on a performance-per-dollar basis, according to Semianalysis (though some dispute the results). Meanwhile, Advanced Micro Devices' (AMD -13.25%) GPUs continue to gain traction. The company has improved its software stack, and its chips' larger memory capacity makes them ideal for inference.

If the gap between AI chips continues to shrink and AI models can be quickly shifted to run on any AI accelerator, this is very bad news for Nvidia. Not only will the company continue to lose market share, but, more importantly, its pricing power will erode, and its margins will shrink. The Nvidia premium can be seen in its gross margins, which are in the mid-70% range, versus AMD’s in the mid-50% range. Meanwhile, all of Nvidia's largest customers, outside of SpaceX (SPCX -7.69%), have been developing their own custom AI chips to try to break free of Nvidia's high prices.

All is not lost for Nvidia, as the company still holds some advantages. It has created a cohesive hardware, software, and networking ecosystem that should still be able to command a premium. It also has major current supply chain advantages, having locked up more access to constrained high bandwidth memory (HBM) supply and having more manufacturing and packaging capacity with Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (TSM -9.48%). That will help it maintain its leadership position, even as it will likely face continued market-share and pricing pressure.

While Nvidia may struggle, these three AI stocks look set to benefit.

Lower AI accelerator prices and more Nvidia GPU alternatives could boost overall AI chip demand, which would benefit Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing. It would also lower TSMC's customer concentration and could actually help with pricing power and gross margins.

Whether a hyperscaler, neocloud, enterprise, or government buys Nvidia GPUs, AMD accelerators, or ASICs through a company like Broadcom (AVGO -12.19%) or Marvell Technology (MRVL -11.69%), TSMC still wins at the end of the day. That makes the stock a buy.

As both a cloud computing giant and a developer of TPUs, Alphabet is one of the best-positioned companies to benefit from the commoditization of AI chips. Alphabet has more than a decade's head start with custom chips and is creating an ecosystem to rival chipmakers like Nvidia and AMD. This lets it get better utilization out of its data centers and gives it an advantage over other companies using their own custom AI chips.

Meanwhile, because its offerings cost much less than Nvidia's, it should be able to gain share with external customers without sacrificing margins. This ultimately could make Alphabet one of the biggest winners in the AI space during this transition.

Starting from a much smaller market-share base with an operating model already built around slimmer gross margins, AMD should benefit as AI chips become more commoditized. Between its lower prices, improving cost performance, and the recent introduction of a complete AI rack system (Helios), AI chip pricing normalization and chip fungibility actually become a tailwind rather than a headwind for a smaller Nvidia competitor like AMD.

Meanwhile, the market is heading in AMD's direction with inference and agentic AI, with customers caring far more about memory capacity, central processing unit (CPU) integrations, and cost-per-action, which is where its chips shine. With its new Helios system, which combines its GPUs and CPUs, AMD has a big opportunity to establish itself as a meaningful alternative to Nvidia in the coming years. This makes the stock a buy.

Indian Auteur Girish Kasaravalli Looks Back on 50 Years of Cinema at IFFI Masterclass: ‘All My Films Talk About Othering’

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Indian auteur Girish Kasaravalli looked back on his career in Kannada-language cinema at the 57th International Film Festival of India (IFFI) in Goa, in a masterclass titled “The Moral Universe of Parallel Cinema: 50 Years of Girish Kasaravalli.”

The 75-year-old spoke about the moral contradictions of society and the relationship between cinematic form and social content. He also argued that even entertainment is a political act. The session was moderated by Variety‘s Asia editor Naman Ramachandran.

Across films such as “Ghatashraddha” (1977), “Tabarana Kathe” (1986), “Thaayi Saheba” (1997), “Dweepa” (2002) and “Gulabi Talkies” (2008), Kasaravalli has kept returning to one question: how societies exclude individuals and communities, and how people who are themselves victims of oppressive structures can also become complicit in perpetuating them.

Asked whether the 1977 Kannada classic “Ghatashraddha,” which examines religious orthodoxy and social exclusion, still spoke to contemporary India, Kasaravalli said: “All my films talk about othering. I always talk about how society looks at some people from a different lens, and in that process they make them ‘other.'” He described this as “the biggest cruelty an individual or a group of people can impart on another person.”

Asked whether he considered himself a political filmmaker, Kasaravalli said: “Whoever makes something, whether he makes mainstream cinema or art cinema, he is making a political statement, either in support of the dominant ideology or opposing the dominant ideology. He cannot avoid it.” There is nothing called only entertainment, he added, and even entertainment is a political form.

On his characters, he said: “I don’t want any of my characters to become a kind of a mouthpiece to a certain ideology.” Drawing on Jean-Luc Godard, he compared understanding a character to buying a house: one must examine it from the outside and then from within before making a judgment. Similarly, a filmmaker must inhabit a character and then step back to assess it. For Kasaravalli, characters are too complex for a filmmaker to easily take sides.

On the evolution of his cinematic language, Kasaravalli described the visual construction of “Ghatashraddha,” as “ritualistic cinema.” “The style is not imposed on it; it comes from within the content itself,” he said. Since “Ghatashraddha” was about a ritual, he felt its cinematic form should reflect that structure.

Kasaravalli said he could not write a script until he had clarity about its images. “For me, the film is not merely making statements; it’s an attempt by the filmmaker to impart an experience.” He described writing detailed instructions for his crew, down to which hand a character uses to lift a glass, because even that changes the composition of a shot.

This attention to the relationship between character and environment shaped “Gulabi Talkies.” Kasaravalli told cinematographer S. Ramachandra that the coastal community had to be as important as the protagonist. He wanted the images to feel like “actuality coverage,” not a predetermined visual construction. Rejecting conventional scenic shots of the sea and rocks, he told his cinematographer: “Film should not have any pretty image.” They chose 16mm, allowing the grain to contribute to the film’s sense of actuality.

The discussion of visual aesthetics brought him to Cuban filmmaker Julio García Espinosa’s idea of “imperfect cinema,” which, for Kasaravalli, challenged the assumption that Western aesthetics should define cinematic perfection. “I don’t want to take your aesthetic sensibility as my sensibility,” he said, quoting Espinosa. Kasaravalli said each country and each region has its own aesthetic concerns. “We should make films according to that condition; we should never imitate the West. Why should I be perfect? I should be loyal to my own milieu.”

Asked whether parallel cinema belonged to a particular period, Kasaravalli disagreed, pointing to notable films being made across the country today. The larger problem, he said, was reaching audiences. Of streaming platforms, he said: “We all thought it is going to be a solution for all of us. But OTT [streaming] is exactly doing what the mainstream distribution network was doing earlier.”

In the audience Q&A, he addressed open endings and whether a filmmaker should provide answers. “A filmmaker who says that he knows answers for all the questions in life is telling a lie,” he said. “He is also as confused as you are. He only throws up options so that you can start thinking. There is nothing called closure… life is always open.”

As the session concluded, IFFI festival director Ashutosh Gowariker described Kasaravalli’s work as “textbooks,” saying he had returned to “Ghatashraddha” while developing a new film. He also singled out the filmmaker’s close-ups. “You let your actors simply be. You make the audience think and anticipate what the character is going through,” Gowariker said.
